Alternatively, the problem now resembles: the total number of distinct elements across all polygons in the interval. 

Each vector v's presence in any polygon in l..r counts towards the total, so it's the count of all vectors present in any of the polygons in the interval.

So to compute this:

Suppose that the vectors are numbered from 1 to M (all possible unique vectors in all polygons), then we can represent for polygon i, the set of vectors it has. The question is: how many vectors exist such that any of the polygons in [L..R] has that vector?

The answer is the union size of all the vectors from the polygons in the interval.

Thus, the disjoint union over the vectors in the polygons from L to R.

The count is the size of the union of all these vectors in that interval.

This problem is the range distinct count problem for a collection of sets. 

This is similar to the problem, for example, given a dynamic sequence of sets, a query asks how many unique elements are there in the union of a subset of those sets. Offline, there are some methods, but for large N, perhaps we need to tackle it with some method.

   402	            ]
   403	
   404	            if el.is_dir():
   405	                folder_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   406	            else:
   407	                file_size = su.get_file_size(el)
   408	                children.append(
   409	                    v.ListItemActionText(class_="ml-1", children=[file_size])
   410	                )
   411	                file_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   412	
   413	        folder_list = humansorted(folder_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   414	        file_list = humansorted(file_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   415	
   416	        parent_item = v.ListItem(
   417	            value=str(folder.parent),
   418	            children=[
   419	                v.ListItemAction(
   420	                    children=[
   421	                        v.Icon(
   422	                            color=self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["color"][v.theme.dark],

   429	                ),
   430	            ],
   431	        )
   432	
   433	        folder_list.extend(file_list)
   434	        folder_list.insert(0, parent_item)
   435

   458	    The relevant columns (lat, long and id) can then be identified in the updated select. Once everything is set, the widget will populate itself with a json dict.

   460

   464<#sep#>#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>
#include <vector>

#define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256

__global__ void softmax_kernel(const float* __restrict__ x, float* __restrict__ y, int num_features) {
    int batch_idx = blockIdx.x;
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int stride = blockDim.x;
    
    const float* x_row = x + batch_idx * num_features;
    float* y_row = y + batch_idx * num_features;
    
    extern __shared__ float sdata[];
    
    // Find max using registers first
    float thread_max = -INFINITY;
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        thread_max = max(thread_max, x_row[i]);
    }
    
    // Reduce max values in shared memory
    sdata[tid] = thread_max;
    __syncthreads();
    
    for (unsigned int s = stride/2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            sdata[tid] = max(sdata[tid], sdata[tid + s]);
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }
    
    float max_val = sdata[0];
    
    // No sync needed here since we're only reading max_val
    
    // Compute exponentials and partial sums using registers
    float thread_sum = 0.0f;
    
    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        float val = __expf(x_row[i] - max_val);
        y_row[i] = val;  // Store intermediate result
        thread_sum += val;
    }
    
    // Reduce sum values in shared memory
    sdata[tid] = thread_sum;
    __syncthreads();
    
    for (unsigned int s = stride/2; s > 0; s >>= 1) {
        if (tid < s) {
            sdata[tid] += sdata[tid + s];
        }
        __syncthreads();
    }
    
    float sum_val = sdata[0];
    
    // No sync needed before normalization since we're only reading sum_val
    
    // Normalize with minimal synchronization
    #pragma unroll 4
    for (int i = tid; i < num_features; i += stride) {
        y_row[i] = y_row[i] / sum_val;
    }
}

void softmax_forward_cuda(const float* x, float* y, int batch_size, int num_features) {
    dim3 block_dim(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);
    dim3 grid_dim(batch_size);
    
    int shared_mem_size = sizeof(float) * THREADS_PER_BLOCK;
    
    softmax_kernel<<<grid_dim, block_dim, shared_mem_size>>>(x, y, num_features);
}

torch::Tensor forward(torch::Tensor x) {
    TORCH_CHECK(x.is_cuda(), "Input tensor must be a CUDA tensor.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.dim() == 2, "Input tensor must be 2D.");
    TORCH_CHECK(x.scalar_type() == torch::kFloat32, "Input tensor must be float32.");
    
    auto y = torch::empty_like(x);
    
    softmax_forward_cuda(
        x.data_ptr<float>(),
        y.data_ptr<float>(),
        x.size(0),
        x.size(1)
    );
    
    return y;
}

PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) {
    m.def("forward", &forward, "Softmax forward (CUDA)");

Hence, the count for [L..R] is the number of vector v for which (min_v <= R) AND (max_v >= L)

Where min_v is the first occurrence (earliest index) of the vector v, while max_v is the last occurrence (latest index).

Therefore, this reformulates the problem to: for a query interval [L, R], count all vectors v where min_v ≤ R and max_v >= L .

If we can precompute for all v the min_v and max_v, then the question is how quickly we can count all vectors v with min_v ≤ R and max_v >= L.

This reduces the problem to a 2D range count: given a set of points (min_v, max_v) for each vector v, count how many points lie within the region where min_v ≤ R AND max_v ≥ L.

 So, the original question's range query [L, R] is equivalent to counting such vectors. 

Now, the problem becomes: given a 2D plane with points (a_i, b_i) for each vector v, where a_i = min_v and b_i = max_v, how many points have a_i ≤ R and b_i ≥ L.

If we can preprocess these points and queries in such a way that we can answer this count quickly, it would solve the problem.

Yes. Thus our goal is to answer a 2-dimensional range count: number of points where x = a_i <= R, y = b_i >= L.

To perform this efficiently, it's better to pre-process the points in a way that allows us to query this efficiently.

There are different approaches to solving such 2D range counting:

1. Offline approach using plane sweep and events sorted in a particular manner. 

2. Using a 2D prefix array with BIT/segment tree, but it may be difficult due to the coordinate ranges.

2) Since the coordinates are X = min_v (up to n=1e5) and Y= max_v (up to 1e5), we can map these coordinates to rank orders. However since the numbers can be as big as 1e5, they can be kept as is.

An efficient data structure for this is a 2D Fenwick tree, but the problem is the size. A 2D BIT would need O(n^2), which is way too big.

Another idea is to use a BIT on one dimension and a nested array on the other, 

Alternative idea: since the query is for x <= R and y >= L, this can be transformed into querying all points (x, y) in rectangle [0, R] × [L, ∞). Since all y <= n, the second dimension is up to n.

Thus for the first dimension (x) they must <= R and for the second dimension (y) >= L.

 We can structure this as a 2D problem where for each point, we can pre-process a prefix sum. 

This would be possible by using a Plane. If can create a grid, and each cell (x,y) stores whether there's a point here. But with n being 1e5, it's impossible storage-wise. 

Alternative solution:

We can model it as follows: arrange all the points according to their y (max_v) and for each y, we have a list of the min_v. Then, for a query L and R, we need to count the total points with max_v >= L and min_v <= R.

This can be approached by first considering all y (max_v) >= L. 

So the first part is to filter all vectors with max_v >= L. Let's sort all vectors v by their max_v. Then for y >= L, we can look only from the entries where max_v >= L. 

But even better, for a query L:

the max_v must be >= L. So for these vectors, their max_v is at least L. 

The question then becomes, for all those vectors where max_v is in [L, n], how many among them have their min_v <= R.

 So, perhaps first, we can sort all vectors by their max_v, and then for each possible max_v, store a list of their min_v. 

Then, for a query L and R, we start by considering all max_v in [L, n], and count in them the number of min_v that is <= R.

Thus:

Let us create an array of buckets indexed by max_v. Each bucket (say B) will store a list of all vectors v whose max_v is exactly that. 

When we need to find all vectors with max_v ≥ L, we can iterate over all B from L to N, and take all the min_v in those buckets and count how many have min_v ≤ R.

However, even this approach may be slow, unless the per-query computation can be optimized via a frequency array. 

Alternatively, for each possible y value ( which is up to 1e5 ), we can store a list of min_v entries (sorted), and using binary search on those lists to count how many min_v <= R.

 This approach:

- Precompute for each y (max_v value), a sorted list of the min_v values among vectors v where max_v = y.

- Then, for a query (L, R):

    The required count is the sum over y in [
